## AI Research

### [What is the Iliad Intensive?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/moG6k8mJiGvH4zc8j/what-is-the-iliad-intensive)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Almost two months ago, Iliad </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b9bhm2iypgkCNppv4/announcement-iliad-intensive-iliad-fellowship"><span>announced the Iliad Intensive and Iliad Fellowship</span></a><span>. Fellowships are a well-understood unit, but what is an </span><i><span>intensive</span></i><span>? This post explains this in more detail!</span></p><p><b><span>Comparison.</span></b><span> The Iliad Intensive has similarities to </span><a href="https://www.arena.education/"><span>ARENA</span></a><span>, but focuses more on foundational AI alignment research instead of alignment research engineering. Expect more math and less coding.</span></p><p><b><span>Rhythm.</span></b><span> It’s currently four weeks long. Five days a week. 10am till 6pm every day, with lunch and an afternoon break. This makes for around 6.5 hours of learning a day, which is at the upper end of how long most people can concentrate deeply within a day. This is why we call it “Intensive”.</span></p><p><b><span>Content.</span></b><span> The Iliad Intensive is broken into five clusters, with 20 total modules, one for each day. The clusters and modules in the April iteration are below. We expect to add substantially more topics and material over the coming months. There is much more material than can be covered in a single month, so different Intensives will vary in content.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Alignment Cluster</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>AI Alignment: an Introduction</span></li><li value="2"><span>Alignment in Practice</span></li><li value="3"><span>AI Alignment: The Field</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Learning Cluster</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Deep Learning 1</span></li><li value="2"><span>Deep Learning 2</span></li><li value="3"><span>Singular Learning Theory</span></li><li value="4"><span>Training Dynamics</span></li><li value="5"><span>Data Attribution</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Interpretability Cluster</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Intro to ML Engineering</span></li><li value="2"><span>Mechanistic Interpretability</span></li><li value="3"><span>In-Context Learning and Belief State Geometry</span></li><li value="4"><span>Abstractions and Latents</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Agency Cluster</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>Reinforcement Learning</span><span>	</span></li><li value="2"><span>Idealized Agency: Coherency and AIXI</span></li><li value="3"><span>Agent Foundations</span></li><li value="4"><span>Reward Learning theory</span></li><li value="5"><span>World Models</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Safety Guarantees and their Limits</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="6"><ul><li value="1"><span>Debate</span></li><li value="2"><span>Steganography and Backdoors</span></li><li value="3"><span>Worst-case Interpretability and Heuristic Arguments</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>We will share the entire curriculum of the April iteration at the start of May, alongside a reflection of how the program went. In the meantime, </span><a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/1p1uVembOX_g6OA1UbHwePG36reRRu4E2/view?usp=sharing"><span>here</span></a><span> you can find a problem sheet that formed the theoretical component for the day on reinforcement learning. The students had the choice between working through this or working through </span><a href="https://learn.arena.education/chapter2_rl/01_intro_rl/"><span>ARENA’s RL intro day</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><b><span>A typical day.</span></b><span> We do not yet have a set daily structure. We may narrow down more in the future based on student feedback, but currently, we are experimenting with the following types of sessions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Internal lectures and expert guest lectures;</span></li><li value="2"><span>Reading sessions: Students read a paper or blogpost;</span></li><li value="3"><span>Whole-class discussions and small-group discussions, with and without discussion prompts;</span></li><li value="4"><span>Math exercise sessions, alone and in pairs;</span></li><li value="5"><span>Coding sessions, alone and in pairs.</span></li></ul><p><span>Our impression so far is that students like exercises and coding and, broadly, a variety of different activities on a day.</span></p><p><b><span>Students selection. </span></b><span>We mainly look for mathematical expertise in students, which typically comes from having a degree in maths, physics, or theoretical computer science. We also look for research experience, general competence, and the motivation for pursuing our program. </span></p><p><b><span>Practical Logistics.</span></b><span> The program currently runs in-person in London, and we consider running future programs also in the Bay area. We provide a fixed stipend of $5000, which can be used by the students to pay for travel and housing. We also provide office space, and lunch and dinner for five days per week.</span></p><p><b><span>The team. </span></b><span>The Iliad Intensive is organized by </span><a href="https://www.iliad.ac/"><span>Iliad</span></a><span>, an umbrella organization for applied math in AI alignment that also runs </span><a href="https://www.iliadconference.com/"><span>a conference series</span></a><span>, incubates a new </span><a href="https://blog.alignmentjournal.org/coming-soon/"><span>Alignment journal</span></a><span>, and has the ambition to incubate new AI alignment research bets similar to </span><a href="https://timaeus.co/"><span>Timaeus</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.simplexaisafety.com/"><span>Simplex</span></a><span>. The materials are created by a team of around 15 internal and external researchers who have domain expertise for the relevant module. We will list their contributions in detail once we release the materials.</span></p><p><b><span>Apply. </span></b><span>If the above sounds appealing to you, </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/forms/d/e/1FAIpQLSdowlqgTRI9vvcsNZupfbiqVsp-3Hy0adwvbzJzMRsnq33iSw/viewform" rel="noreferrer"><span>please apply in this form</span></a><span>! The June Intensive runs from Saturday June 6 until Friday July 3 (both inclusive) in London. The deadline for the June Intensive is Wednesday, April 22, EoD.</span></p><p><span>Details of the August Intensive are to be determined, but you can already apply for it in the common form.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/moG6k8mJiGvH4zc8j/what-is-the-iliad-intensive#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The Mirror Test Is Complicated](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5eLoZQshfre8DGaxd/the-mirror-test-is-complicated)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>The Mirror Test is kind of like Hitler. In any discussion of animal cognition, </span><i><span>somebody</span></i><span> is going to bring it up. The conversation usually goes like this:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>A: So, most animals can’t recognize themselves in the mirror</span></p><p><span>B: Which animals specifically?</span></p><p><span>A: Oh, dogs, cats, betta fish, monkeys, that sort of thing. Anyway as I was saying, those animals can’t. But some smart animals </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> recognize themselves in the mirror.</span></p><p><span>B: Such as?</span></p><p><span>A: Well, chimpanzees and orangutans for a start.</span></p><p><span>B: Makes sense</span></p><p><span>A: Not gorillas though, at least not always. But dolphins and elephants can!</span></p><p><span>B: Yeah, those animals are smart as well</span></p><p><span>A: Magpies can, though crows cant.</span></p><p><span>B: Sure, ok</span></p><p><span>A: And cleaner wrasse can as well.</span></p><p><span>B: The uhh, finger-sized fish? You sure?</span></p><p><span>A: Yeah. And also ants.</span></p><p><span>B: What.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>What?</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!3nVw!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F7dd02fae-1f5a-478b-afc0-854e896a1326_270x186.jpeg" target="_blank"><img alt="Mirror-guided self-decoration by an ape Suma, an orangutan at a German... |  Download Scientific Diagram" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5eLoZQshfre8DGaxd/hy373vfsrklidkmhdpc0" /></a></p><p><span>Frans de Waal drew this picture of an orangutan putting lettuce on her head and then actually got it </span><a href="https://www.researchgate.net/figure/Mirror-guided-self-decoration-by-an-ape-Suma-an-orangutan-at-a-German-zoo-often_fig4_330943479" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>published in a real journal</span></a><span>. Based.</span></p><h2><span>What do we actually mean by the “Mirror Test”</span></h2><p><span>“The mirror test” elides a bit of a distinction between different kinds of test. There’s lots of things you can do which look like “put an animal in front of a mirror and see what happens” and they give slightly different answers.</span></p><p><span>Sometimes, an animal will just treat its reflection as a same-sex conspecific (i.e. a member of its own species and sex) which usually means trying to fight the reflection. This typically goes poorly, but is slightly funny to watch. This is generally considered a failure.</span></p><p><span>Other times, an animal will behave differently in front of its own reflection, compared to how it would behave with a same-sex conspecific. Monkeys typically behave a bit weirdly. But are they recognizing how a reflection works, or just wondering why they’re being copied?</span></p><p><span>The gold standard is the mark test. Put a white mark on an elephant’s face, without it knowing. Then put it in front of a mirror. The elephant will clean the mark off its face (and won’t do this if you just pretend to mark them). This is considered pretty damn strong evidence that the animal “gets” a mirror.</span></p><p><span>This works for magpies as well (which groom themselves with their feet) and orangutans, which have hands. You may see a problem with it already…</span></p><h2><span>The Complicated Ones</span></h2><p><span>The mark test specifically requires animals to actively groom themselves. Some animals just don’t care. Pigs, for example, are very smart and can use mirrors as a kind of tool, but since they don’t care about having a mark on their faces (nor could they really do anything about it (no hands)) the mark test is basically inconclusive.</span></p><p><span>Bottlenose dolphins will </span><i><span>look</span></i><span> at the mark in the mirror, but again, they don’t have any way to groom themselves, so how would we know if they </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> got what was going on.</span></p><p><span>Then there’s some interesting cases: gorillas can </span><i><span>kinda</span></i><span> figure out what’s going on but they’re also super aggressive. Monkeys will use a mirror to groom an area they’re already investigating, but won’t groom a mark they didn’t know was there.</span></p><h2><span>The Unbelievable Ones</span></h2><p><span>In that I struggle to believe them.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2208420120" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Cleaner wrasse</span></a><span> are finger-sized fish which feed on parasites found on larger fish. They have a kind of grooming-like behaviour, which consists of rubbing themselves against a rock in order to dislodge a parasite. They do this when marked and presented with a mirror. Huh.</span></p><p><span>Then it gets, well, unbelievable. Apparently they can, having seen their reflection once, </span><i><span>remember</span></i><span> their own appearance. They demonstrate this by showing the fish a photograph of itself with a mark on it, to which the fish responds by performing its grooming behaviour. Huh?</span></p><p><span>The authors also show that the fish don’t respond this way to altered photos of other fish, </span><b><span>and</span></b><span> manage to isolate the effect to the </span><i><span>face</span></i><span> of the image by creating composite head/body images with marks!</span></p><p><span>And some </span><a href="https://difusion.ulb.ac.be/vufind/Record/ULB-DIPOT:oai:dipot.ulb.ac.be:2013/219269/Holdings" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>ants</span></a><span> also passed the classic mirror test with flying colours: grooming themselves only when marked, and when placed in front of a mirror. They specifically groomed themselves when the mark was in a location that was visible in the mirror, and not when it was on their backs (the ants were walking around on top of the mirror). They only groomed the appropriate parts of their body, and only when the mark was a visible colour.</span></p><p><span>The most baffling thing of all, however, is the fact that when re-introduced to their original ant pals, the marked ants were often murdered!</span></p><h2><span>Making Sense Of It All</span></h2><p><span>What cognitive mechanisms allow an animal to pass the mirror test? Well, they have to:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Notice that their reflection behaves differently to other same-sex conspecifics</span></li><li value="2"><span>Map their own sensorimotor responses onto the reflection, and notice that it behaves like their own body</span></li><li value="3"><span>Have a model of the world which contains a map of their own body, and figure out that they’re looking at a map of their own body</span></li><li value="4"><span>Connect the mark on the image to the mark on their own body</span></li><li value="5"><span>Actually care enough to engage in grooming behaviour</span></li></ol><p><span>This totally makes sense for chimpanzees. They have complex, flexible interactions with other chimps, so can easily notice that their reflection is behaving differently to a normal same-sex conspecific. They almost certainly have a mental map of their own body, and can map it to the mirrored reflection.</span></p><p><span>Some people, like Eliezer Yudkowsky, have used the mirror test as a proxy for self-awareness, but I’m not sure it’s slam-dunk. Self awareness is about modelling one’s own mind, whereas the mirror test only really requires an animal to have a model of its own body.</span></p><p><span>Let’s go back to the cleaner wrasse: I think it’s kind of interesting that the main test we use (will an animal clean itself) is being passed by an animal </span><i><span>whose job it is to clean!</span></i><span> This can’t be a coincidence! Their brains are highly specialised to recognise other fish’s bodies, and locate and remove remove parasites from them.</span></p><p><span>On the other hand, there’s an even crazier explanation. Cleaner wrasse are constantly in a game theoretic problem with their “client” fish, which are often large and predatory. The smaller wrasse could easily be eaten by the larger fish (if they caught them) yet the wrasse will often swim into their mouths to clean their teeth! Maybe the cleaner wrasse are using logical decision theory, which requires them to have an understanding of the location of their own cognitive algorithm in the world.</span></p><p><span>Ok, so the cleaner wrasse are probably not using logical decision theory, and neither are the ants. While cleaner wrasse do seem to have an intricate social structure, revolving around politics between individual bands, this isn’t quite the same as how chimpanzees work. Ants definitely don’t have complex social interactions: their social interactions are about as simple as it can possibly get.</span></p><p><span>Overall, I’d guess that the mirror test isn’t </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> good as a test of the kinds of self-awareness that (might) really matter for things like consciousness. You only need a map of your own body, not one of your own mind, in order to pass it.</span></p><p><i><span>Editor’s note: this post was written as part of Doublehaven (unaffiliated with Inkhaven)</span></i></p><p><span>◆◆◆◆◆|◆◆◆◆◆|◆◆◆◆◆</span><br /><span>◆◆◆◆◆|◆◆◆◆◆|◆◆◆◆◇</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5eLoZQshfre8DGaxd/the-mirror-test-is-complicated#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Nectome: All That I Know](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3i5GMhpGbDwef9Rns/nectome-all-that-i-know)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>TLDR:</span></b><span> I flew to Oregon to investigate </span><a href="https://nectome.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Nectome</span></a><span>, a brain preservation startup, and talk to their entire team. They’re an ambitious company, looking to grow in a way that no cryonics organization has before. Their procedure is probably much better at saving people than other orgs, and is being </span><a href="https://nectome.substack.com/p/preservation-pre-sales"><span>offered</span></a><span> for as little as </span><b><span>$20k until the end of April</span></b><span> — a (theoretical) 92% discount. (I bought two.) This early-bird pricing is low, in part, due to some severe uncertainties, in both the broader world and in Nectome's ability to succeed as a business.</span></p><p><b><span>Meta:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I'm </span><a href="https://maxharms.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Max Harms</span></a><span>, an AI alignment researcher at </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>MIRI</span></a><span> and author.</span></li><li value="2"><span>This deep-dive only assumes </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functionalism_(philosophy_of_mind)"><span>functionalism</span></a><span> and a passing familiarity with cryonics, but no particular knowledge of Nectome.</span></li><li value="3"><span>I have been a cryonics enthusiast for my whole adult life, and that is probably biasing my views, at least a little. I want Nectome to succeed.</span></li><li value="4"><span>That said, I am also a rationalist, and I have worked very hard to set aside my wishful thinking and see things with cold objectivity.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Throughout the essay, I've attached explicit probabilities for my claims in parentheticals. You can click these probabilities to access Manifold markets so we can bet.</span></li><li value="6"><span>This essay is titled "Nectome: All That I Know," but technically I know a few things that were off the record and that I agreed not to share. I do not think they change the overall picture very much.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvms4682jgm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvms4682jgm">[1]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><b><span>Contents:</span></b></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The Problem</span></li><li value="2"><span>The History</span></li><li value="3"><span>The Team</span></li><li value="4"><span>The Plan</span></li><li value="5"><span>The Money</span></li><li value="6"><span>The Future</span></li><li value="7"><span>The Bottom Line</span></li></ol><hr /><h1><span>1. The Problem</span></h1><p><span>Cryonics has a big problem.</span></p><p><span>The most basic, high-level story says: the patterns that make up a human mind don’t disappear instantly when someone’s heart stops beating, but rather when their brain decomposes. If we reduce the temperature of a brain, this halts decomposition, and gives time for medical technology to progress to the point of potentially being able to rescue the person before their pattern is truly lost. Unfortunately, dropping the temperature low enough that the tissue is stable over many years requires going far below freezing, and the formation of ice crystals in flesh also causes horrible damage. To prevent ice, cryoprotectant is perfused through the brain, replacing the water in cells with a solution that (at least ideally) doesn’t freeze, but instead turns into a glass-like “vitrified” state. There are a lot of pesky details that I’m glossing over, including some serious problems, and I’ll get back into the nitty gritty science in a bit. But none of this is the </span><i><span>big </span></i><span>problem with cryonics.</span></p><p><span>The big problem with cryonics is that </span><b><span>most of the time when someone who signed up for cryonics is pronounced legally dead, a critical window of time passes where their brain deteriorates before the cryonics provider is able to operate.</span></b><span> The main culprit here is ischemia — oxygen starvation — which dramatically changes the character of the network of blood vessels that run through the brain. In a working brain, these capillaries are open and clear, able to pump blood through every part of the brain. During a cryonics procedure, these same pathways are used to push cryoprotectant into the tissue, and ferry away the excess water. But when oxygen is cut off, the endothelial cells swell,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhwg67qyqj3p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhwg67qyqj3p">[2]</a></sup></span><span> shrinking the already-narrow passages, trapping cells, and causing clots. In the literature, this one-way, serious damage to the circulatory system is called the “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_reflow_phenomenon"><span>no-reflow phenomenon</span></a><span>” and at normal temperatures it begins after just a few minutes.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref89jrdzcjhko"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn89jrdzcjhko">[3]</a></sup></span><span> By </span><a href="https://www.jacc.org/doi/10.1016/j.jacbts.2022.06.008"><span>the 15 minute mark</span></a><span>, even on blood thinners, most of the brain will be more-or-less permanently cut off.</span></p><p><span>Rapidly cooling the brain can stretch this out. There are </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_B%C3%A5genholm"><span>famous cases</span></a><span> of people falling into freezing water and surviving at least an hour with no blood flow to their brains.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj5wtxdez02"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj5wtxdez02">[4]</a></sup></span><span> So, in principle, someone might be perfusable if they are rapidly chilled, have external life support circulating oxygenated blood, and only a few hours pass before perfusion. But this is an idealized case, and not how things go much of the time. Most cryonics clients wait </span><i><span>over a day</span></i><span> between legal death and being cryopreserved (</span><a href="https://manifold.markets/MaxHarms/did-most-cryopreserved-humans-go-24" rel="noreferrer"><span>90%</span></a><span>), and in many cases, people spend </span><i><span>hours at warm temperatures</span></i><span> after legal death but before any real action is taken.</span></p><p><span>Some information about these poor souls is probably saved. It can actually take quite a while for the structure of the neurons to fall apart, and the technologies of the future will almost certainly look miraculous. If you want someone to be preserved, cryonics is clearly better than nothing. But "some information is saved" is a far cry from "the person is still intact." Most critically, with significant delay between legal death and perfusion, I am highly skeptical that the typical cryonics client's brain is adequately perfused in the regions that matter most (</span><a href="https://manifold.markets/MaxHarms/the-typical-cryonics-brain-is-wellp" rel="noreferrer"><span>15%</span></a><span>). The brain's finest blood vessels — small penetrating end-arteries with no backup routes — are the first to be permanently blocked by ischemic clotting and blockage, and they feed broad swaths of cortex and white matter. Once the brain is dropped below freezing, I suspect that these vital areas are then shredded by ice.</span></p><p><span>We need to do much, much better.</span></p><h2><span>How to Fix Cryo</span></h2><p><span>The most important thing that an individual can do to maximize their chances is to legally die inside or right next to the facility where they’re going to be perfused. They should do this quickly,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbxd0i6lnf6p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbxd0i6lnf6p">[5]</a></sup></span><span> at an expected time, and with a team of professionals standing by, ready to act with utmost urgency. In other words, if you want to maximize your chances of being saved, you should probably get Medical Assistance in Dying (MAiD), where a doctor prescribes drugs that can let you legally end your life on your own terms. </span></p><p><span>The good news is that this is possible. MAiD is available in </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_suicide_in_the_United_States"><span>more than a dozen US jurisdictions</span></a><span>, including Oregon, California, Washington, New York, D.C., and Vermont. Sadly, it’s not available in either Arizona or Michigan, where Alcor and the Cryonics Institute are based, respectively, so you’ll either need to be perfused away from their main facility (uncommon) or transported after death (hard) or hope that you legally die quickly in hospice at the right time without medical assistance (risky). Outside the USA, MAiD is available in Canada, The Netherlands, Switzerland, Spain, Austria, and much of Australia. If you don’t live in one of those places, don’t fret — Oregon, Vermont, and Switzerland allow MAiD for non-residents.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8idj3enlih"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8idj3enlih">[6]</a></sup></span><span> And while MAiD is controversial, its availability is undeniably growing over time.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776118699/lexical_client_uploads/oqt8bci6e2dm495tbaer.png" /><p><span>In the USA, to take advantage of MAiD, one needs to already be dying. In Oregon, for example, MAiD can only be provided to people who have been prognosed with less than six months left to live. But this applies more often than you might think. </span><a href="https://allianceforcareathome.org/2025-facts-and-figures-report-now-available-to-alliance-members/"><span>About half</span></a><span> of people who die on Medicare do so in hospice, which also requires a less-than-six-month-life-expectancy prognosis. For those who are willing to shop around for pessimistic doctors</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq8y7b6cxeb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq8y7b6cxeb">[7]</a></sup></span><span> when they get deeply ill, I estimate that maybe 3/4ths of people in the USA could theoretically take advantage of MAiD, setting aside financial obstacles.</span></p><p><span>(MAiD, for the record, is not particularly expensive, at least in the world of healthcare. The medications are self-administered and cost about </span><a href="https://www.aadm.org/patientinfo"><span>$700</span></a><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh94cozq8sf8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh94cozq8sf8">[8]</a></sup></span><span> and must usually be purchased out-of-pocket. The physician fees are more variable. Medicare and most health insurance covers end-of-life care, including consultation, but Medicare at least won’t pay for anything specifically about MAiD.)</span></p><p><span>Beyond the individual, the biggest thing that cryonics needs is </span><i><span>scale.</span></i><span> Right now, if you want to save your loved one, you need to convince doctors, hospital administrators, and funeral directors to do something weird. In a world where minutes matter, we can’t afford to explain why rapidly cooling the head is so important. We need paramedics who continue CPR after someone is declared legally dead. We need a perfusion facility in every hospital. We need a world where, in the most stressful moments of a person’s life, they can let the professionals take over, rather than fighting an uphill battle to break norms without coming across as crazy and stricken with grief.</span></p><p><span>In addition to these benefits, I believe scale can also make the process of legally dying with MAiD much safer. Right now, cryonics teams are unable to really act until a doctor/nurse/EMT pronounces someone dead, and while the cocktail of ingested drugs used in places like Oregon are almost always painless,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefim2azbrp0dc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnim2azbrp0dc">[9]</a></sup></span><span> it can sometimes take hours before legal death is declared. During this window of dying, the body is changing, and it’s a somewhat open question of what can go wrong during that period, from a cryonics perspective. With increased scale comes awareness and advocacy such that more places can become like Canada in allowing physicians to administer the drugs intravenously, and thus speeding things along compared to the oral route. One can even imagine a world where life-support machines are used to provide oxygenated blood to the brain, even as the patient’s heart is stopped and they’re declared legally dead.</span></p><p><span>But for cryonics to reach that kind of scale, there needs to be a cryonics company that is ambitiously trying to grow. And unfortunately, </span><b><span>the field of cryonics has historically been extremely averse to growth.</span></b><span> Alcor and the Cryonics Institute (CI) are the two biggest players in the field, each with approximately 250 people preserved over about 50 years — an average of only about 5 people per year! The field has grown a little bit since the 70s, but the total number of people getting cryopreserved across the whole world each year is almost certainly less than 50. These cryonics companies are not-for-profit, and aim for keeping costs as low as possible "for the public good." This means setting aside next-to-nothing for advertising/marketing,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9jg804yjurh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9jg804yjurh">[10]</a></sup></span><span> running into </span><a href="https://www.cryonicsarchive.org/library/cryopreservation-funding-and-inflation/" rel="noreferrer"><span>funding issues</span></a><span> when inflation causes negotiated contracts to fail to cover costs,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhbxzd91sd3t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhbxzd91sd3t">[11]</a></sup></span><span> and generally failing to invest in growth.</span></p><p><span>If only someone would step up and actually try to change the world…</span></p><h1><span>2. The History</span></h1><h2><span>The Brain Preservation Foundation</span></h2><p><span>In the late 2000s, there was an emerging zeitgeist in neuroscience: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connectomics"><span>connectomics</span></a><span>. Instead of thinking mainly in terms of connections between large-scale brain regions, scientists were starting to be able to use scanning techniques and computers to build models of brains that included details for individual neurons, and even individual synapses. In 2010 — the same year that </span><a href="https://www.ted.com/talks/sebastian_seung_i_am_my_connectome"><span>connectomics made a splash at the TED conference</span></a><span> — Ken Hayworth, who had completed his PhD in neuroscience at USC the year before, was living in the Boston area and working in the lab of Harvard professor Jeff Lichtman, one of the main connectomics pioneers.</span></p><p><span>Hayworth was excited about the potential for preserving people’s connectomes after death, but was unconvinced by what he saw in the field of cryonics. He knew what well-preserved tissue looked like and didn’t think that standard methods of cryopreservation were anywhere near good enough. Taking inspiration from another bit of 2010’s zeitgeist, he co-founded the Brain Preservation Foundation (BPF) and offered a pair of incentive prizes — for preserving both small and large mammal brains — to anyone who could prove that they were </span><i><span>actually </span></i><span>capturing the vital synaptic information.</span></p><p><span>Here’s </span><a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/ken-hayworths-personal-response-to-mit-technology-review-article/"><span>Hayworth in 2015</span></a><span> (emphasis added):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I started the Brain Preservation Prize as a challenge to Alcor and other such companies to ‘put up or shut up’, challenging them to show that their methods preserve the synaptic circuitry of the brain. </span><b><span>After five years they have been unable to meet our prize requirements even when their methods were tested (by a third party) under ideal laboratory conditions.</span></b><span> Out of respect for loved ones I will not comment on any particular case, but it is clear from online case reports that their </span><b><span>actual results are often far worse than the laboratory prepared tissue we imaged.</span></b><span> Speaking personally, I wish that all such companies would stop offering services until, at a minimum, they demonstrate in an animal model that their methods and procedures are effective at preserving ultrastructure across the entire brain. By offering unproven brain preservation methods for a fee they are effectively making it impossible for mainstream scientists to engage in civil discussion on the topic.</span></p></blockquote><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775848224/lexical_client_uploads/ady8vzv5kioc41qqfodv.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Serious neuroscientist Ken Hayworth </span><i><span>rejects </span></i><span>your weaksauce cryonics! Shame! Do better!</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Aurelia Song</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflukpw6fm4tg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlukpw6fm4tg">[12]</a></sup></span><span> was a graduate student working on a master’s in EECS from MIT</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefabilu4hozk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnabilu4hozk">[13]</a></sup></span><span> who became interested in the BPF . She began to volunteer there, first doing things like working on their website, fundraising, and doing outreach. But in 2015, in the midst of learning about the science, she started to think that she saw a path towards claiming the prizes. At heart, it was simple: neuroscientists </span><i><span>already had</span></i><span> techniques that preserved brain tissue well. This is what Hayworth was using as a gold standard! With a few changes, those same techniques could be used to win.</span></p><p><span>But to claim the prize, Song needed the help of someone who had actually trained in the field, and while she was on good terms with Hayworth, there was an obvious conflict of interest, given that he was one of the judges for the prize. So Aurelia Song reached out to 21st Century Medicine (21CM), the California-based cryobiological research company that had developed M22, the cryoprotectant that has been used by Alcor since 2005. 21CM has had a complex relationship with the cryonics community for decades, fundamentally working in the same space (and often towards the same ends), but trying to keep its distance from the immortality-flavored transhumanist rhetoric, and focusing instead on things like cryo-stabilized organ transport and other, more respected, technologies. They initially weren’t very interested in Song’s proposal, but she persisted, and was eventually hired to work with cryo pioneer, Greg Fahy.</span></p><p><span>Fahy and Song soon developed </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S001122401500245X"><span>aldehyde-stabilized cryopreservation</span></a><span>, a synthesis of standard cryonics techniques (cryoprotectant + extremely low temperatures) and standard neuroscience techniques (glutaraldehyde — a cousin of formaldehyde that essentially glues proteins and other large molecules firmly in place). In 2016, </span><a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/small-mammal-announcement/"><span>they won the BPF prize</span></a><span> for small mammals, showing state-of-the-art preservation of nano-scale details of a rabbit brain.</span></p><p><span>About that same time, Song and her fellow MIT alum and ex-roommate, Michael McCanna, founded a startup, Nectome, with the bold mission of taking that same technique to human clients. By early 2018, Song and Fahy had </span><a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/large-mammal-announcement/"><span>won the second BPF prize</span></a><span>, this time for a pig brain, demonstrating that high-quality preservation of humans was indeed on the horizon. Nectome was set to revolutionize human brain preservation.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775848431/lexical_client_uploads/jmxx6kc9slyh6veeohl2.png" /><h2><span>Media Missteps</span></h2><p><span>Before 2018, Nectome had basically been a tiny, stealth-mode startup. But almost immediately after winning the large mammal prize, Nectome debuted as part of Y Combinator’s 2018 winter cohort… and proceeded to stumble into a media firestorm. To this day, if you Google “Nectome” the second result, after their website, is </span><a href="https://www.technologyreview.com/2018/03/13/144721/a-startup-is-pitching-a-mind-uploading-service-that-is-100-percent-fatal/"><span>this early-2018 article</span></a><span> from MIT Technology Review with the headline: “A startup is pitching a mind-uploading service that is ‘100 percent fatal’”.</span></p><p><span>To state the obvious, Nectome is not “pitching a mind-uploading service” nor even a service that is “100% fatal,” regardless of what Song said in 2018. Nectome is a company that takes people </span><i><span>who are already dying,</span></i><span> and allows them to try to preserve as much of themselves as possible, so that some </span><i><span>other </span></i><span>party can potentially use that preserved information in the future, including </span><i><span>possibly </span></i><span>creating an upload based on the person’s preserved brain. Yes, for people like me and Aurelia Song, uploading to extend lifespan is the obvious use-case, but I think it’s clear in retrospect that if Nectome had been marketing itself better in those early days, it would have leaned into a more pluralistic/agnostic position as to why someone would want to buy their services. They might’ve talked about the value we get from mummies and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96tzi"><span>ancient frozen people</span></a><span>, or of </span><a href="https://www.britannica.com/story/what-is-jeremy-benthams-auto-icon"><span>Jeremy Bentham’s</span></a><span> decision to be preserved for the benefit of others. They might have leaned on the way in which we don’t know what future technology might look like. And they certainly could’ve much more clearly emphasized that they are trying to save and extend life, rather than end it.</span></p><p><span>But that’s not how things went. Instead, the media smelled blood in the water and focused on weirdness and controversy. Many outlets featured quotes by naysayers that didn’t really address the core idea. A few weeks later, the MIT Media Lab issued a </span><a href="https://www.media.mit.edu/posts/the-media-lab-and-nectome/"><span>public statement</span></a><span> cutting ties to Nectome, which the broader media tended to characterize as the whole of MIT condemning the company.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775848535/lexical_client_uploads/ohfm9cfqwmolwir48di0.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>This meme was featured prominently on a </span><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2018/03/13/nectome/"><span>TechCrunch article</span></a><span> from the time.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Still, Nectome went on. They received about two million dollars in a combination of pre-seed/seed investments and federal grants. Later in 2018 they made their first hire, Jessica Radley, who is now the CEO. She, alongside the founders, worked to repair Nectome’s reputation and build skill in talking about their business with those who weren’t already on board with the premise.</span></p><p><span>I don’t know the details, but I speculate that the struggles of 2018 were particularly hard for Michael McCanna, and that it was during this time that he really began to drift away from Song. McCanna started </span><a href="https://theorg.com/org/immunefi/org-chart/michael-mccanna"><span>advising</span></a><span> an AI-centric YC startup in 2019. Like Song, he studied computer science at MIT, and left the company a year later to eventually become COO at </span><a href="https://immunefi.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Immunefi</span></a><span>, a crypto-security company.</span></p><h2><span>The Long, Slow Science of Getting the Details Right</span></h2><p><span>Then Covid hit.</span></p><p><span>Song had been preserving brains</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefus39saep3l9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnus39saep3l9">[14]</a></sup></span><span> for nearly half a decade, but these were always a proof of concept. To get a mature process that was good enough to use on real human clients, more work was needed, and with the pandemic driving everyone into lockdown and shaking up the status quo, the decision was made to pare the company down to just Aurelia Song. McCanna went off to do tech things. Jessica Radley left to go live on a boat. It was a weird time.</span></p><p><span>But through it all, Song persisted. Buoyed by the initial investment round, and driven by a vision of how to potentially save millions of lives, she moved to Oregon and continued to iterate towards a procedure that would work. With the help of a couple assistants, she continued to practice on pigs and on donated human cadavers. In 2021 it took her, with the help of two assistants, twenty-two minutes to preserve a pig. Now it’s closer to five minutes</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9npmce98fjf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9npmce98fjf">[15]</a></sup></span><span> — fast enough to perfuse the entire brain before ischemia ruins things.</span></p><p><span>As anyone who has worked in science, engineering, or medicine will tell you: there’s a big difference between a proof-of-concept and a real-world technique that can work with actual human beings. For instance, it took Song’s team months simply to experimentally determine which filters to use for the circulatory pumps. Everything from the placement of the cannula tubes to the exact method of surgery needed to be learned through the basic methods of science and engineering. Nectome is doing something that nobody has ever done before, and that requires diligence and determination. Thankfully, they seem to have both.</span></p><p><span>Nectome’s team grew slowly. Additional angel investments came in, here and there. Andrew Critch carefully ran an </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NEFNs4vbNxJPJJgYY/nullius-in-verba-3rd-party-evidence-for-nectome-s-brain#In_the_field__Andrew_Critch_and_the_SFF"><span>independent review</span></a><span> of their technique, leading to another half-million dollars from the Survival and Flourishing Fund.</span></p><h1><span>3. The Team</span></h1><p><span>A few weeks ago, on March 25th, I flew up to Portland</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefitvaxnenwab"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnitvaxnenwab">[16]</a></sup></span><span> to visit with their entire team, ask questions, and tour their lab. Based on that limited visit, here’s my sense of the people involved:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Aurelia Song, Founder and CTO, President of the Board of Directors</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>Passionate, talented, and dedicated, Aurelia is the beating heart of Nectome. She clearly knows her science, and is an extremely nerdy rationalist who isn’t afraid to say what she believes and go against “common wisdom.” Her ambition and idealism perhaps get the better of her sometimes, but those same traits make her a good startup founder, and don’t get in the way of her skill as a scientist and engineer.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Charlie Todd, Operations</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>I only met Charlie briefly, but they were my first point of contact via email and have shown up in the comments a </span><i><span>lot </span></i><span>on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/aurelia"><span>Aurelia’s LessWrong posts</span></a><span>. They came across as energetic, engaged, and friendly, which seem like great traits to have when fielding inquiries from the public.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Anna LaVergne, Lab Tech</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Anna is a local hire from the Portland region who learned about Nectome via a posting on Astral Codex. Anna also gave me strong rationalist vibes, albeit from a quieter, more skeptical angle than Aurelia. During a lull in talking about Nectome, I asked everyone except Charlie (who had a prior commitment) what they thought about AI, timelines, doom, et cetera. Opinions were all over the place, but Anna was probably the closest to my pessimistic-MIRI position. When I was getting the lab tour, she seemed to really know her stuff, and she’s clearly on track to be one of the primary people who operates on clients. I would feel good in her hands.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Borys Wróbel, Chief Science Officer</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>Borys is Nectome’s serious, graybeard scientist. Born in Poland, he got his PhD in Biology in 1998 and was working on brain preservation in the Netherlands as Scientific Director of the European Institute for Brain Research before coming to America to work with Nectome. He was a delightful contrast to the rationalist/tech-startup/American vibes coming from the rest of the company, and I think his presence as CSO is a good sign for Aurelia’s taste in people and the seriousness of Nectome as an organization. We talked for a while about the brain preservation situation in Europe, and why things are more dysfunctional there than might be apparent from afar, as well as the publication pipeline for Nectome’s </span><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.03.04.709724v1"><span>journal preprint</span></a><span> of their preservation techniques.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Jessica Radley, CEO, Board Member</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnc5hdvdsrel"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnc5hdvdsrel">[17]</a></sup></span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="6"><ul><li value="1"><span>During my visit, I spent the most time with Jessica, who rejoined the company last year to become CEO. She has a background in AI (back in the days before LLMs took off), drives a Mustang, and used to live in a Bay-Area group house with a bunch of consciousness hackers.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4jv5ek54wmg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4jv5ek54wmg">[18]</a></sup></span><span> She was the only member of the team that gave me real </span><i><span>business </span></i><span>vibes, and was probably the person at Nectome who most impressed me, due to her clear combination of truly believing in the mission (and in Aurelia) alongside a sober awareness of how their success or failure will ride at least as much on the confidence of investors and clients as it does on the technical skill and scientific prowess of the team. Unlike the others, she was clearly guarded around me, recognizing that this essay wouldn’t necessarily paint them in a wholly positive light, and (correctly!) trying to manage me as something like a journalist, rather than as a curious new friend.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>Nectome Needs More Businesspeople</span></h2><p><span>Radley is — as fitting for the CEO — probably the most important person at the company right now. To grow, and achieve the dream of scale, Nectome needs to metamorphose from being a wonky research lab to being a real business that’s capable of courting investors, government officials, the media, the broader public, and especially their customers. Song is great for explaining the science and vision, especially to nerdy transhumanists like me, but for these more outward-facing relationships, she needs help. One of the best signs about Nectome is that Song appears to really understand this, putting Radley in charge and giving her a strong vote of confidence. And conversely, one of my biggest concerns is that I don’t see Radley as having much of a digital presence or taking much of a front-and-center role as a figurehead. Before meeting the team, I wasn’t even sure if Radley was still working at the company, or if Song was still the CEO.</span></p><p><span>All that is fixable, of course, and I did get the feeling that Radley has what it takes to succeed, but her situation seems to me to mirror the state of Nectome as a whole: lots of potential that has yet to be proven.</span></p><p><span>Take, for example, the stark absence of anyone on staff with a background in running a business, marketing, sales, advertising, media relations, law, policy, finance, or accounting. Yikes! They assured me that they have legal consultants in Oregon, California, and D.C., but having lawyers who you can pay to answer questions is a very different thing than having someone whose full-time job is to make sure Nectome doesn’t get sued. They also have several advisors who are surely helping, but again, that’s very different from someone working day-to-day to ensure success. A new method of brain preservation is not an easy thing to sell, and based on how 2018 went, I would’ve hoped for an entire comms </span><i><span>team</span></i><span> as part of their debut, rather than one energetic ops person.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5jb6bmr16g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5jb6bmr16g">[19]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>It’s understandable that these people need to get hired at some point, and apparently that point is now/soon, but unlike the average small business, I claim Nectome cannot simply post job openings and hire the most qualified people that walk in the door. Nectome is a tech/innovation startup looking to break into untapped markets and change the world. As such, culture and alignment are vital to hiring their first wave of businesspeople. If the initial marketing, legal, and finance hires are true normies who don’t believe in Nectome’s mission, that will ultimately doom the company to lots of internal friction and lack of coordinated effort. Song has done a great job picking people so far, but scientists are more in her wheelhouse than salespeople, and it’s not even clear to me that good people for these roles </span><i><span>exist.</span></i></p><p><span>(Speaking of which, if you’re excited about brain preservation, work in any of those areas, and are looking for a job, you might want to reach out to </span><a href="mailto:hello@nectome.com"><span>hello@nectome.com</span></a><span> !)</span></p><h1><span>4. The Plan</span></h1><p><span>Nectome is not aiming to squeeze into the same, existing niche as orgs like Alcor. Nectome is </span><i><span>ambitious</span></i><span>. They’re aiming to bring brain preservation to the mainstream. In the comments to her post, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E9xfgJHvs6M55kABD/less-dead"><i><span>Less Dead</span></i></a><span>, Song </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E9xfgJHvs6M55kABD/less-dead?commentId=HqWWna92bECLJDiCQ"><span>writes</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I think we will be getting to thousands of preservations per year in a few years</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This is, I think, wildly over-optimistic (</span><a href="https://manifold.markets/MaxHarms/will-nectome-perform-1000-brain-pre-NLEAPP8EzA" rel="noreferrer"><span>94%</span></a><span>), and probably won’t even be true by 2040 (</span><a href="https://manifold.markets/MaxHarms/will-nectome-perform-1000-brain-pre" rel="noreferrer"><span>75%</span></a><span>). But it might make sense as a </span><i><span>target</span></i><span>. By aiming high, Nectome is thinking about scale in a way that no other brain preservation org is, as far as I can tell. This means they have some hope of succeeding, even if it takes longer than they hope.</span></p><p><span>When I visited, they were in the middle of trying to find a house in the Portland area to buy and turn into the inaugural Nectome brain preservation center, where their first clients could come and be preserved. At first, I thought the idea of doing the procedure in a converted house was a bit strange. Shouldn’t there be some fancy facility, like a hospital or assisted-living-type place? If they’re aiming for scale, their facility should be big!</span></p><p><span>But this is wrong for a few reasons. First, hospitals and assisted living facilities are set up to house people for extended periods. In most cases, Nectome is envisioning the dying client traveling out to their center, spending a couple days there with their family, then the operation happens and everyone leaves. It needs to be comfortable enough to host people for short periods of time, and big enough to hold one or maybe two families, as well as having space for the procedure. But Song’s procedure doesn’t actually require much equipment — Song first practiced it on pigs out of the back of a converted U-Haul truck. And Nectome is very conscious of the need to give things the right vibe. As Song explained to me, “People want to die peacefully in their living rooms, surrounded by family.” Nectome might not be able to make house calls,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref37evv5yy1u9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn37evv5yy1u9">[20]</a></sup></span><span> but they can provide </span><i><span>a</span></i><span> peaceful, comfortable living room, which is a better experience for most people than a cold, sterile hospital.</span></p><p><span>And, importantly, houses are relatively cheap. Buying or constructing a big facility is costly, both in terms of money, time, and flexibility of location. There are very few restrictions on where someone can get MAiD, or where Nectome is able to do a perfusion, and with the ability to quickly acquire and convert residences, this first center in Oregon is intended to be one of many. While states like California aren’t ideal starting places, they do have MAiD laws, and Nectome is clearly hoping to set up preservation centers in the bay area, the east coast, Canada, and beyond.</span></p><h2><span>MAiD Services</span></h2><p><span>So, let’s imagine your parent, grandparent, or some other loved one is dying. Their doctor says they have stage-4 cancer or something equally dire. You tell them about Nectome, and instead of spending hundreds of thousands of dollars fighting for the chance to extend their remaining days into some number of years of potentially very-low-quality life, they decide that being preserved is the more hopeful, life-affirming path.</span></p><p><span>To make this happen, they will need to go through a few steps in Oregon (or, more ideally, in their home state, if it supports MAiD and Nectome has expanded there). Because many places have laws forbidding MAiD, telemedicine probably won’t be an option in the near future.</span></p><p><span>So your loved one flies out to Oregon and rents a hotel or AirBnB or whatever for a few weeks. To help them along the path, Nectome might put them in touch with a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_doula" target="_blank"><span>death doula</span></a><span> and/or give them a list of doctors who are known to be comfortable with Nectome and MAiD. Once in Oregon, they’ll need to find and meet with an administering physician who gives a prognosis of less-than-six-months-left-to-live, at which point they then need to verbally request MAiD and have that physician agree to participate. Afterward, they’ll need to find and meet with an independent doctor who gives a similarly terminal prognosis and agrees to participate in the role of consulting physician.</span></p><p><span>(If either physician thinks that the dying person is psychologically unwell, things get significantly more complicated, but MAiD is still possible with the involvement of a psychiatrist and support from family.)</span></p><p><span>Once both physicians have signed on and explained what’s involved and laid out the various available alternatives, a written request must be filled out and witnessed. Then, at least two days after this paperwork is complete and fifteen days</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefset9m6z89yf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnset9m6z89yf">[21]</a></sup></span><span> after the first verbal request, they must meet with the attending physician again and verbally confirm that they want to be prescribed life-ending medication. Only now can a participating pharmacy sell the necessary cocktail, which must be mixed by the patient, but which may be taken at any time.</span></p><p><span>It’s at this point that they head to the Nectome preservation center for up to an up-to-two night stay, along with family/caregivers. Paperwork is filled out, granting Nectome rights to their body after legal death and interviews are conducted (which we’ll revisit in a bit). Perhaps this is when you fly out to see them and say goodbye, if you weren’t staying with them beforehand and helping with the process.</span></p><h2><span>Sendoff</span></h2><p><span>The preservation center is</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnpidppqiee"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnpidppqiee">[22]</a></sup></span><span> in an unassuming house in a quiet part of Oregon. (Probably in the Portland suburbs?) You and the other loved-ones all gather around the person who is dying in what is basically a living room. There are lots of seats. It’s quiet and comfortable. You might even watch a favorite episode of some familiar TV show while you wait for the designated hour, the family dog curled up on their lap. As the time approaches, a nurse practitioner from the patient's care team arrives, as do the relevant Nectome staff.</span></p><p><span>After goodbyes are said, most of the family is shepherded to another room. The dying person mixes and drinks the prescribed medication</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw5zwl1mb2b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw5zwl1mb2b">[23]</a></sup></span><span> and the nurse sits next to them to monitor vitals. Nectome’s team waits nearby, with an open doorway to something like an operating room. After a few minutes, the sedatives in the cocktail cause them to fall asleep, and their breathing slows. A tense waiting period follows where the nurse carefully tracks their heartbeat. After perhaps </span><a href="https://www.oregon.gov/oha/PH/PROVIDERPARTNERRESOURCES/EVALUATIONRESEARCH/DEATHWITHDIGNITYACT/Documents/year23.pdf"><span>50 minutes</span></a><span>, the nurse practitioner pronounces them legally dead.</span></p><p><span>Nectome springs into action, moving the client into the operating room and leaving the remaining family, death doula, and nurse behind. Surgery begins immediately, connecting cannulas to the heart’s arteries to begin the blood washout and perfuse the whole body with aldehydes.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefck5u2ubcl67"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnck5u2ubcl67">[24]</a></sup></span><span> While it might seem faster to cannulate the carotid arteries, Song's experience is that it's not — the vertebral arteries are also needed for good human perfusion, and the delicacy of working on the neck ends up slowing things down. The difference in cost in perfusing just the brain vs the entire body is negligible.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefb4tjv5k4j7c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnb4tjv5k4j7c">[25]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>And, as a nice benefit, the client is now stable </span><i><span>at room temperature.</span></i><span> Are they stable for years? Maybe! There aren’t good studies on how much vital information is lost, when neural tissue is stored at room temperatures for long periods. But based on what data we do have, the body will at least be stable for weeks.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6t499jwafv4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6t499jwafv4">[26]</a></sup></span><span> This is, in fact, a similar procedure to what is done in a traditional funeral home to prepare for an open-casket funeral, and Nectome is prepared to work with funeral directors to allow for their clients to get open-casket funeral services afterwards.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj8ecec6e5c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj8ecec6e5c">[27]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>Storage</span></h2><p><span>After the funeral, Nectome takes possession again, and moves the client’s body to a long-term storage facility (not the preservation center). If this is in the next few years, the facility is probably something like a warehouse in the Portland area. It might even be a truck with specialized refrigeration capability. If we’re looking further out, and Nectome is on the path to success, the storage facility is potentially more like a low-temperature mausoleum set up somewhere in a stable place in the far north, such as Canada, Sweden, or Norway.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8cf5zklwd18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8cf5zklwd18">[28]</a></sup></span><span> (See also: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svalbard_Global_Seed_Vault"><span>The Svalbard Global Seed Vault</span></a><span>.) From what I understand, it is not that difficult, legally, to transport bodies</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5z0j288ci8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5z0j288ci8">[29]</a></sup></span><span> to whatever part of the globe is safest for their clients.</span></p><p><span>Compared to traditional cryonics, the storage phase for Nectome is considerably easier, because while Nectome does perfuse the brain with cryoprotectants that can help prevent freezing if they go cryogenic, </span><b><span>Nectome does not intend to store its clients at true cryogenic temperatures.</span></b><span> Instead of vitrifying at -135°C or -196°C temperatures using liquid nitrogen, Nectome intends to use standard industrial freezers to store their clients at closer to -30°C (-22°F). Thanks to the cryoprotectant, this is above the temperature where there’s any risk of ice crystal formation, and comes with several benefits:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Liquid nitrogen (LN2) is more expensive.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>An Alcor Bigfoot dewar boils off about </span><a href="https://www.cryonicsarchive.org/library/allocation-of-long-term-care-costs-at-alcor/" rel="noreferrer"><span>10 liters per day</span></a><span>, and they probably pay about $0.5/liter. If we suppose that one Bigfoot has 10 clients,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8196t4dli9u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8196t4dli9u">[30]</a></sup></span><span> that’s (conservatively) about $180/year per client, just from cooling.</span></li><li value="2"><span>By contrast, Nectome can theoretically insulate an entire room and take serious advantage of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square%E2%80%93cube_law" rel="noreferrer"><span>the square-cube law</span></a><span>. Back-of-the-envelope says a purpose-built structure in Canada could easily preserve 10k clients for less than $10,000 in annual energy costs. Even if mostly empty, that's only $10/year/client.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrspz306rx8f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrspz306rx8f">[31]</a></sup></span><span> With more optimistic assumptions we can even imagine costs closer to &lt;$1/year/client.</span></li><li value="3"><span>A more basic industrial freezer could probably hold 30 clients, while consuming less than $3,000/year in energy — $100/year/client. Nectome doesn’t have to scale huge before they start having a price edge.</span></li><li value="4"><span>While these dollar figures aren’t huge, they can matter a lot in terms of how much money needs to be set aside per client to pay for long-term support.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>LN2 requires more of a supply chain, which can be broken, and it limits where you can put your facility.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Imagine you want to store clients in Svalbard. During winter it is hard to reach the island, and 1% daily boil-off makes shipping LN2 long distances expensive. Better to manufacture your own, at the facility.</span></li><li value="2"><span>But while LN2 is one of the easier substances to produce (cool+compress the ambient air, then separate) this still requires specialized machines that need to be sourced from afar.</span></li><li value="3"><span>In a global emergency, such as a world war, this machinery might be hard to get, and you thereby run the risk of not having what's needed to keep the clients safe.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>More generally, industrial freezers are a simple technology and are easy to source/scale.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Nectome wants to grow quickly, and this path helps facilitate that. No specialized dewars need to be designed or manufactured. Suitable, large-scale freezers capable of holding dozens of clients can cost </span><a href="https://www.barrinc.com/coolers-freezers/walk-in-coolers/"><span>less</span></a><span> than </span><a href="https://gzkoller.com/the-cost-of-walk-in-coolers/"><span>$30k</span></a><span> and can be purchased today.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Perhaps most importantly, </span><b><span>storing at deep-cryo temperatures is dangerous.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>At around -135°C, objects like vitrified brains are essentially solid glass. As the temperature continues to drop towards the -196°C temperature of LN2, the glass contracts, with the outside contracting faster than the inside. This uneven contraction creates mechanical stress, and can even cause shattering if done too quickly.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7on1xorom36"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7on1xorom36">[32]</a></sup></span><span> The inverse problem can also happen if the solidified tissue warms up too quickly.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Unfortunately, storing at -135°C and/or carefully controlling the cooling rate requires an expensive cryogenic freezer, whereas dropping the temperature rapidly and hoping for the best is cheap.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Even tissues perfused with cryoprotectant run </span><a href="https://nectome.substack.com/p/some-thoughts-on-nectomes-long-term" rel="noreferrer"><span>some risk</span></a><span> of ice crystal formation when the temperature drops from -40°C to -130°C or vice-versa. Due to a dynamic where ice crystals </span><i><span>nucleate</span></i><span> on the colder end and then ice </span><i><span>grows</span></i><span> on the warmer end, it's particularly dangerous to warm up from cryogenic temperatures slowly. But if you do it too quickly, you get stress fractures!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftap7jffz7mm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntap7jffz7mm">[33]</a></sup></span><span> By storing the brain above the phase transition temperature, there is no ice risk from warming and Nectome can avoid this double-bind.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>As part of transporting the client to their facility, Nectome also then hands off legal control to a distinct, non-profit organization</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref05vslmfvz28w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn05vslmfvz28w">[34]</a></sup></span><span> that’s dedicated to the long-term care of preserved people. Like the cryonics nonprofits, this nonprofit manages an endowment trust for each patient, and pays for the storage facility’s upkeep using the returns from a diversified portfolio of low-risk investments.</span></p><p><span>It’s hard to predict exactly how much each client’s endowment will need before the details of Nectome’s long-term storage facility and nonprofit wing are firmly established. Wealthy clients will almost certainly be able to donate to the nonprofit to ensure they will have enough support, but a lot depends on the trajectory of the future. As I understand it, Nectome </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E9xfgJHvs6M55kABD/less-dead?commentId=HqWWna92bECLJDiCQ"><span>intends</span></a><span> to invest approximately 100x the annual storage costs in the seed endowment of typical clients, which will mean the endowments almost certainly will grow over time, if the future looks anything like the last few hundred years.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5tp6js7aavg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5tp6js7aavg">[35]</a></sup></span><span> My best estimate is that seed funding for endowments will be about $30k.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8biivrs7bde"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8biivrs7bde">[36]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftun5ap54k8q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntun5ap54k8q">[37]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Returning to our narrative, what should we expect for the future of your preserved loved-one? Well, back when they were making final arrangements with Nectome, they did a series of interviews. One such interview was to check that they were a good fit for Nectome, understood the plan and the risks involved, were mentally able to consent, and so on. But another set of interviews were meant to document the client’s wishes for how they wish to be handled as time goes on and the world changes.</span></p><p><span>For example, if technology is developed that appears to allow preserved people to be rescued, do they want to be an early-adopter, or wait until it’s well established? If rescue involves being uploaded into a computer, and no biological revival is yet possible, do they want to do the upload, or wait? Are there any countries they would never want their remains to enter? If the world looks like it’s coming undone, due to war, tyranny, AI, or something else, would they like to be cremated, just in case there is a fate worse than death? Is there anything else they want the future to know, in case they can’t be revived?</span></p><p><span>In my time there, I got the impression that Nectome will take the preferences of their clients very seriously. During the long wait after legal death, a significant part of how things go will, if Nectome is in good shape, be steered by the preferences and plan that their client lays out beforehand.</span></p><h2><span>Messy MAiD</span></h2><p><span>So that’s the plan. Overall, I think it’s pretty good, and that the success or failure of Nectome will mostly hinge on business details, price points, and whether there’s enough of a market. (Oh, also on whether AI is about to upend the whole world, which I’ll get into towards the end of the essay.) But one major technical detail stands out to me as a pain-point: the slowness and messiness of MAiD, particularly in terms of the ingested cocktail used in Oregon.</span></p><p><span>Nectome has never actually done their procedure. They have proven success on pigs. But as I understand it, they did not use ingested MAiD drugs to kill their pig subjects. The pigs were healthy and young, not suffering from dementia and atherosclerosis. It seems very plausible (</span><a href="https://manifold.markets/MaxHarms/in-2029-will-at-least-75-of-nectome" rel="noreferrer"><span>45%</span></a><span>) to me that, in practice, things will go wrong in at least a quarter of cases and full perfusion will be impossible.</span></p><p><span>I asked Song about this, and she didn’t seem to have a good answer. She’s truly dedicated to saving people, and aware that situations are likely to emerge where Nectome will simply need to do their best to preserve whatever they can as best they can. But having an intention is different from having battle-hardened skill in navigating messy situations, and my bet (</span><a href="https://manifold.markets/MaxHarms/will-at-least-4-of-nectomes-first-2" rel="noreferrer"><span>75%</span></a><span>) is that Nectome will lose significant parts of many of their early clients as they learn by doing and gain the experience needed to handle edge-cases.</span></p><p><span>To be clear: this doesn’t mean going through Nectome’s procedure is worse than traditional cryonics. I think Nectome’s procedure is likely the best option, even given the team’s lack of experience. My point is that it still feels like a huge gamble, at least right now. In ten years (assuming they’re still doing preservations), my guess is it’ll be a lot safer and a lot clearer what the major risk-factors are.</span></p><p><span>An ideal change would be for Oregon to change their laws to allow direct injection of the drugs by a physician, like in Canada, Spain, and The Netherlands. (And even more ideally, with a circulatory machine running while legal death is pronounced.) Alternatively, other places that allow a speedier end could allow non-residents in the same way that Oregon currently does, and Nectome could set up preservation centers in those jurisdictions. I’m particularly hopeful about Switzerland, which seems to have a relatively sane outlook towards people having freedom and dignity in how they leave the world.</span></p><h1><span>5. The Money</span></h1><p><span>Let’s transition away from thinking about cryonics as a procedure and more about it as a business, starting with prices. Nectome is launching with a price point of $250,000 per person. That’s a lot of money! But is it an unreasonable amount?</span></p><p><span>Average medical spending in the last year of life in the USA is </span><a href="https://www.richmondfed.org/-/media/richmondfedorg/publications/research/working_papers/2018/pdf/wp18-18.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>about $112k</span></a><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdza20ht4c1u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndza20ht4c1u">[38]</a></sup></span><span> going up to $217k if we look at the last three years of life. Of these costs, </span><a href="https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lanam/article/PIIS2667-193X(22)00176-4/fulltext" rel="noreferrer"><span>about 85%</span></a><span> are covered by a mixture of insurers and government programs like Medicare, making the average out-of-pocket costs at the end of life ~$15k. From this perspective, Nectome’s price point is quite high, especially when considering that Nectome’s price doesn’t include the doctor visits, death doula, transportation, or funeral expenses.</span></p><p><span>Still, there is a heavy right-tail in medical spending, and old people often have a lot of money! According to </span><a href="https://dqydj.com/net-worth-by-age-calculator/"><span>the 2022 Survey of Consumer Finances</span></a><span>, the </span><i><span>median</span></i><span> person in all 65+ age demographics in the USA has a net worth of over $300k. The top quartile has between 2.5 and 3 million dollars, and, of course, we’re not even getting into the top 1%. From this perspective, Nectome is a modestly-priced luxury — out of reach of many, but still arguably (barely) within reach of most Americans.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775859240/lexical_client_uploads/kqhdve9uzpztcoqy6ujq.png" /><p><span>As a rough guess, not having done in-depth market research,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq9gp4hol93n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq9gp4hol93n">[39]</a></sup></span><span> I believe that this is a reasonably smart price-point, in terms of maximizing revenue. Nectome has serious potential to be head-and-shoulders better, in terms of preservation quality, than their competition. This edge in quality positions them well to establish themselves as a premium brand, like Gucci, Rolex, and Ferrari.</span></p><p><span>When a premium brand is top-of-the-line, my sense is that they should largely choose their price point to simultaneously have huge margins on each customer, while keeping the pool of potential customers large enough to keep them busy. Arguably, they might be better-off charging </span><i><span>even more.</span></i><span> There are a lot of eccentric millionaires around the world!</span></p><p><span>Nectome is aware of this, and is gearing up to present themselves as a top-of-the-line option, targeting their marketing effort towards wealthy clients.
